1	import { PGlite } from "@electric-sql/pglite";
2	import { db } from "../db.js";
3	import { readFileSync } from "fs";
4	import { join } from "path";
5	import { beforeEach, afterEach } from "vitest";
6	
7	const schema = readFileSync(join(import.meta.dirname, "..", "schema.sql"), "utf-8");
8	
9	let pglite;
10	
11	function createPgliteSql(pg) {
12	  return function sql(strings, ...values) {
13	    let text = "";
14	    strings.forEach((str, i) => {
15	      text += str;
16	      if (i < values.length) text += `$${i + 1}`;
17	    });
18	    return pg.query(text, values).then((r) => r.rows);
19	  };
20	}
21	
22	beforeEach(async () => {
23	  pglite = new PGlite();
24	  db.sql = createPgliteSql(pglite);
25	  await pglite.exec(schema);
26	  await pglite.query("INSERT INTO users (username, password_hash) VALUES ($1, $2)", [
27	    "testuser",
28	    "hashed_password_123",
29	  ]);
30	});
31	
32	afterEach(async () => {
33	  await pglite.close();
34	});
35	
```

Good, you're right about postgres.js — its tagged template literals handle parameterization automatically, so item 6 is already covered by the library.

Let me ask you a few things to figure out where to start:

Looking at the todo list, items 1, 5, and 6 seem covered. That leaves:
- Item 2: `favorites` table in the schema
- Item 3: `db/build.sql`
- Item 4: `db/build.js`

**Question 1:** You already have `src/schema.sql` with the `users` table. The todo also asks for a `db/build.sql`. Do you think those should be *two separate files*, or is `src/schema.sql` meant to *become* `db/build.sql`? What's the difference in purpose between them?

**Question 2:** Looking at what `src/tests/favorites.test.js` expects from a POST to `/api/favorites` — it sends `entity_id`, `entity_type`, and `title`, and expects those back plus an `id`. What columns do you think the `favorites` table needs? And what constraint should `entity_type` have, given the test also expects a 400 when it's `"movie"` instead of `"anime"` or `"manga"`?

```
1	# Project: Anime Showcase & Search
2	
3	Express server serving 3 static HTML/JS/CSS pages, proxying data from the [Jikan API](https://docs.api.jikan.moe/).
4	
5	## Pages & Routes
6	
7	| Route        | File                       | Purpose           |
8	| ------------ | -------------------------- | ----------------- |
9	| `/`          | `public/index.html`        | Top anime home    |
10	| `/search/`   | `public/search/index.html` | Search by name    |
11	| `/anime/:id` | `public/detail/index.html` | Anime detail view |
12	
13	## File Structure
14	
15	```
16	public/
17	  index.html        ← top anime home page
18	  app.js
19	  style.css
20	  search/
21	    index.html
22	    app.js
23	    style.css
24	  detail/
25	    index.html
26	    app.js
27	    style.css
28	src/
29	  app.js            ← Express app, serves public/ via express.static
30	  server.js         ← starts server on port 9080
31	  router.js         ← API routes + manual route for /anime/:id
32	  db.js             ← exports db.sql tagged template (postgres.js in prod, PGlite in test)
33	  schema.sql        ← database schema (users table)
34	  controllers/
35	    top-anime.js
36	    anime-search.js
37	    anime-details.js
38	  tests/
39	    setup.js        ← global test setup (PGlite per test, schema, seed)
40	    search.test.js
41	    db.test.js
42	    stubs/
43	      search-one-piece.json
44	vitest.config.js    ← wires setup.js for all tests
45	```
46	
47	## Tech Constraints
48	
49	- **No async/await** — `.then()` / `.catch()` Promise chains only
50	- **No frontend framework** — vanilla HTML, CSS, JS
51	- **No build tools** — files served as static assets
52	
53	## Architecture
54	
55	**Proxy pattern**: frontend never calls Jikan directly. All API calls go through the Express server.
56	
57	| Express route            | Jikan endpoint    |
58	| ------------------------ | ----------------- |
59	| `/api/top-anime`         | `GET /v4/top/anime` |
60	| `/api/search?q=...`      | `GET /v4/anime?q={query}` |
61	| `/api/anime-details/:id` | `GET /v4/anime/{id}` |
62	
63	- Routes use kebab-case
64	- Detail page URL: `/anime/:id` — Express serves `public/detail/index.html` via manual route in `router.js`
65	- Frontend reads anime ID from `window.location.pathname.split("/")[2]`
66	- Cards on home and search pages navigate to `/anime/{mal_id}`
67	- URL encoding via `encodeURIComponent` on search and detail pages
68	
69	## Jikan API
70	
71	Base URL: `https://api.jikan.moe/v4`
72	
73	Response shape:
74	- Top-level keys: `pagination`, `data`
75	- Anime array at `response.data`
76	- Each anime: `title`, `score`, `mal_id`, `images.jpg.image_url`
77	
78	## Tests
79	
80	- Framework: Vitest + supertest
81	- Global setup: `src/tests/setup.js` (configured in `vitest.config.js`)
82	- Stubs in `src/tests/stubs/` (real Jikan response shapes)
83	- Test names describe user behavior, not implementation
84	- Each test validates: status code, content type, response body shape
85	
86	### Database in tests
87	
88	- Each test gets a fresh in-memory PostgreSQL via [PGlite](https://pglite.dev/)
89	- `src/tests/setup.js` handles `beforeEach`/`afterEach`: creates PGlite instance, runs `src/schema.sql`, seeds a test user (`testuser` / `hashed_password_123`), and swaps `db.sql` to a PGlite-backed wrapper
90	- Controllers use `db.sql` tagged template — same interface in prod (postgres.js) and test (PGlite wrapper)
91	- **Never mock `db.sql`** — queries run against real SQL in PGlite
92	- To add tables: update `src/schema.sql`, they'll be created automatically each test
93	
94	### Mocking external APIs
95	
96	- **Only mock external HTTP calls** (Jikan API), never database calls
97	- Mock pattern: `vi.stubGlobal("fetch", () => Promise.resolve({ ok: true, json: () => Promise.resolve(stub) }))`
98	- Import stubs: `import stub from "./stubs/search-one-piece.json" with { type: "json" }`
99	- No external API calls in tests — always mock fetch
100	
101	## Remaining Work
102	
103	- [ ] Caching on Express routes (avoid Jikan rate limits)
104	- [ ] Unhappy path tests (rate limit simulation, invalid ID)
105	- [ ] CSS styling for all 3 pages
106	
```

1	import { describe, test, expect, vi, afterEach } from "vitest";
2	import request from "supertest";
3	import { app } from "../app.js";
4	
5	// The database is NOT mocked — setup.js wires db.sql to a fresh PGlite
6	// instance before each test and seeds testuser (password=[REDACTED]").
7	// Only external HTTP calls (Jikan) are ever mocked here.
8	
9	describe("POST /api/auth/register", () => {
10	  afterEach(() => {
11	    vi.restoreAllMocks();
12	  });
13	
14	  test("returns 400 when username is empty", () => {
15	    return request(app)
16	      .post("/api/auth/register")
17	      .send({ username: "", password: "secure123" })
18	      .expect(400)
19	      .expect((res) => {
20	        expect(res.body.message).toMatch(/username/i);
21	      });
22	  });
23	
24	  test("returns 400 when password is shorter than 8 characters", () => {
25	    return request(app)
26	      .post("/api/auth/register")
27	      .send({ username: "alice", password: "short" })
28	      .expect(400)
29	      .expect((res) => {
30	        expect(res.body.message).toMatch(/password/i);
31	      });
32	  });
33	
34	  test("returns 409 when username is already taken", () => {
35	    // "testuser" is seeded by setup.js before every test, so we can rely on it existing
36	    return request(app)
37	      .post("/api/auth/register")
38	      .send({ username: "testuser", password: "secure123" })
39	      .expect(409)
40	      .expect((res) => {
41	        expect(res.body.message).toMatch(/taken|already exists/i);
42	      });
43	  });
44	
45	  test("creates user and returns 201 on valid input", () => {
46	    return request(app)
47	      .post("/api/auth/register")
48	      .send({ username: "alice", password: "secure123" })
49	      .expect(201)
50	      .expect((res) => {
51	        expect(res.body).toHaveProperty("username", "alice");
52	        // Passwords and hashes must never appear in the response
53	        expect(res.body).not.toHaveProperty("password");
54	        expect(res.body).not.toHaveProperty("password_hash");
55	      });
56	  });
57	});
58	
59	describe("POST /api/auth/login", () => {
60	  test("returns 400 when password field is missing", () => {
61	    return request(app).post("/api/auth/login").send({ username: "testuser" }).expect(400);
62	  });
63	
64	  test("returns 401 when username does not exist", () => {
65	    return request(app)
66	      .post("/api/auth/login")
67	      .send({ username: "nobody", password=[REDACTED]" })
68	      .expect(401)
69	      .expect((res) => {
70	        // Generic message — don't reveal whether it was the username or password that was wrong
71	        expect(res.body.message).toMatch(/invalid credentials/i);
72	      });
73	  });
74	
75	  test("returns 401 when password is wrong", () => {
76	    return request(app)
77	      .post("/api/auth/login")
78	      .send({ username: "testuser", password: "wrongpassword" })
79	      .expect(401)
80	      .expect((res) => {
81	        expect(res.body.message).toMatch(/invalid credentials/i);
82	      });
83	  });
84	
85	  test("returns 200 with user info and sets a session cookie on valid credentials", () => {
86	    return request(app)
87	      .post("/api/auth/login")
88	      .send({ username: "testuser", password=[REDACTED]" })
89	      .expect(200)
90	      .expect((res) => {
91	        expect(res.headers["set-cookie"]).toBeDefined();
92	        expect(res.body).toHaveProperty("username", "testuser");
93	        // Hash must never leave the server
94	        expect(res.body).not.toHaveProperty("password_hash");
95	      });
96	  });
97	});

Good, you're right about postgres.js — its tagged template literals handle parameterization automatically (you're already using it correctly in `db.test.js`). So item 6 is covered.

Looking at Phase 1, here's what's left:

| Item | Status |
|------|--------|
| 1. PostgreSQL setup | done |
| 2. Favorites table in schema | **not done** — `schema.sql` only has `users` |
| 3. `db/build.sql` | **not done** |
| 4. `db/build.js` | **not done** |
| 5. postgres.js connection | done |
| 6. Parameterized queries | done (postgres.js) |

Let's start with **item 2** — the `favorites` table.

Your `db.test.js` already tells you exactly what columns it expects. Look at the INSERT on line 47-49:

```
INSERT INTO favorites (user_id, entity_id, entity_type, title)
```

And RETURNING gives back an `id` too.

**Question:** What columns does your `favorites` table need, and what types would you give each one? Think about:
- Which column is the primary key?
- Which column references the `users` table (foreign key)?
- How would you restrict `entity_type` to only allow `'anime'` or `'manga'`? (Hint: SQL has a `CHECK` constraint for this)

Go ahead and add the `CREATE TABLE` to `src/schema.sql` — then we can verify by running the db tests.

> AGENT

You've got the right columns listed — that's the right thinking! But the SQL syntax isn't complete yet. Each column needs a **type** and the table needs a **primary key**.

Let me ask you a few things:

1. **Types** — Look at how the `users` table defines its columns. Each one has a name, then a type (like `TEXT`, `SERIAL`, `TIMESTAMP`), then optional constraints. What type should `user_id` be? (Hint: it references `users.id`, which is `SERIAL` — what integer type does that correspond to?)

2. **Primary key** — Your `db.test.js` expects `rows[0].id` back from `RETURNING *`. So favorites needs its own `id` column. What pattern did you use for the `users` table's `id`?

3. **CHECK syntax** — You've got the right idea with `CHECK`. The actual SQL syntax looks like:
   ```
   column_name TEXT CHECK (column_name IN ('value1', 'value2'))
   ```

4. **Foreign key** — To link `user_id` to the `users` table, you add `REFERENCES users(id)` after the type.

Try fixing the syntax — use the `users` table above as your template for how to format each line.

Getting closer! The CHECK constraint syntax is correct. A few things to fix:

1. **Missing types** — `user_id`, `entity_id`, and `title` still need types. What type is `users.id`? (`SERIAL` stores as `INTEGER`) — so `user_id` should be `INTEGER REFERENCES users(id)`. What about `entity_id` and `title`?

2. **Missing commas** — Look at the `users` table. Each column line ends with a comma (except the last one). Your favorites table is missing those.

3. **Missing primary key** — Your tests expect `rows[0].id` back. You need an `id` column just like in `users`.

4. **Case consistency** — You have `'Manga'` and `'anime'`. Your tests send `entity_type: "anime"` and `entity_type: "manga"` (both lowercase). The CHECK values need to match exactly what your tests send.

Go fix those four things and I'll check again!

Exactly! Your build script needs to:

But here's a thing to think about — postgres.js tagged templates (`db.sql`) are for parameterized queries like `` sql`SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = ${id}` ``. For running a raw SQL file (multiple statements, no parameters), you'd use the `.file()` method or the `unsafe()` method that postgres.js provides.

Check the [postgres.js docs](https://github.com/porsager/postgres#file) — `sql.file()` can run a `.sql` file directly by path. That would make your build script very short.

**Question:** Where should the build script live? The todo says `db/build.js`. And what should it do after running the schema successfully — just log a message and exit? Think about what happens when the script finishes — does the postgres connection need to be closed?

no database need to be running all the time when site is working

Right — the database connection in `db.js` stays open while the Express server runs. The build script is different: it runs once, sets up the tables, then exits.

So your `db/build.js` needs to:
1. Import `sql` from `db.js`
2. Run the schema file against the database
3. **Close the connection** so the script exits (otherwise it hangs forever)

For step 3, postgres.js has `sql.end()` to close the connection.

Go ahead and create `db/build.js` — think about where to put the `.end()` call. (Hint: it should happen after the schema finishes running, which means it goes in a `.then()`)

Good start — you've got the right pieces in mind. A few issues:

1. **Import path** — `'db.js'` won't resolve. You need a relative path from `db/build.js` to `src/db.js`. Think about the folder structure: you're in `db/`, and `db.js` is in `src/`. So what relative path do you need?

2. **What you import** — Look at `src/db.js` again. It exports `db` (an object), and `sql` lives at `db.sql`. So you'd import `{ db }` and then use `db.sql`.

3. **Running the schema** — You need to actually read and execute the schema file before calling `.end()`. You mentioned `sql.file()` could work, or you could use `readFileSync` + `db.sql.unsafe()` like setup.js does with PGlite's `.exec()`.

4. **`.then()` syntax** — `.then()` chains off a Promise. Right now `sql.end()` isn't chained to anything. The pattern is:
   ```
   somePromise
     .then(() => ...)
     .catch((err) => ...)
   ```

Try fixing those things — get the import right first, then the schema execution, then the `.end()` in a `.then()`.

**Step 3** — Run the SQL. postgres.js has `db.sql.unsafe()` which executes a raw SQL string (no parameters). So:
```
db.sql.unsafe(schema)
```
That returns a Promise.

**Step 4** — Chain `.then()` to log success and close the connection, and `.catch()` to log errors and close.

Good progress! You've got the structure right. Let me point out the issues:

1. **Import name** — `db.js` exports `{ db }`, not a default. So it should be `import { db } from "../src/db.js"`

2. **Missing `join` import** — You're using `join()` but didn't import it. Add `join` from `"path"` (same as setup.js does).

3. **Path typo** — You wrote `".src/schema.sql"` — that dot before `src` makes it a hidden folder name. It should just be `"src"`, `"schema.sql"` as separate arguments to `join`. Look at how setup.js does it:
   ```
   join(import.meta.dirname, "..", "schema.sql")
   ```
   From `db/build.js`, you go up one folder (`".."`), then into `"src"`, then `"schema.sql"`.

4. **`.then()` syntax** — `.then` needs parentheses and an arrow function:
   ```
   .then(() => db.sql.end())
   ```

5. **`.catch()` body** — The arrow function is empty. Log the error and close the connection there too.

Fix those five things and check again!

> AGENT

Here's everything we changed:

### 1. `src/schema.sql` — Added the `favorites` table

This is where your database structure lives. You already had the `users` table. Now you added:

- **`id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY`** — auto-incrementing unique ID for each favorite, same pattern as `users.id`
- **`user_id INTEGER REFERENCES users(id)`** — a foreign key linking each favorite to a user. `REFERENCES` tells PostgreSQL "this value must be a valid `id` from the `users` table"
- **`entity_id INTEGER`** — the MAL ID of the anime/manga (e.g. `20` for Naruto)
- **`entity_type TEXT CHECK(entity_type IN ('manga','anime'))`** — a CHECK constraint that rejects any value that isn't `'manga'` or `'anime'`. This is database-level validation
- **`title TEXT NOT NULL`** — the name of the anime/manga, can't be empty

### 2. `db/build.js` — New file, database build script

This is a one-time script to set up the real PostgreSQL database. It:
1. Imports the postgres.js connection from `db.js`
2. Reads `schema.sql` with `readFileSync`
3. Runs the raw SQL using `db.sql.unsafe()` (needed because tagged templates can't run multi-statement SQL files)
4. Closes the connection with `db.sql.end()` so the script exits
5. Has `.catch()` to log errors if something goes wrong

### 3. `src/tests/setup.js` — Minor whitespace change

Just a removed blank line between `beforeEach` and `afterEach`. No functional change.

bcrypt is there. But I notice there's no session/cookie package — your `auth.test.js` expects `set-cookie` in the response and `favorites.test.js` sends a session cookie for protected routes.

**Question:** How do you want to handle sessions? There are two common approaches:
1. **`express-session`** — stores sessions server-side, sets a cookie automatically
2. **JWT** — token stored in a cookie, no server-side storage

The todo mentions "cookie or JWT". Which approach are you thinking? (Note: `express-session` is simpler and your tests already expect a `set-cookie` header)

> DEVELOPER

but jwt is better right ?

> DEVELOPER

but jwt is better right ?

> AGENT

It depends on the use case — neither is universally "better."

**JWT** is better when:
- You have multiple servers/microservices (stateless, no shared session store)
- You're building an API consumed by mobile apps

**express-session** is better when:
- You have a single Express server (which you do)
- You want easy session invalidation (logout = delete session)
- Simpler to implement

For your project — a single Express server with cookie-based auth — `express-session` is the simpler and more practical choice. JWT adds complexity (token expiry, refresh tokens, storage decisions) that you don't need here.

Your tests already expect `set-cookie` headers and send cookies back with `sessionCookie` — that maps perfectly to `express-session`.

Want to go with `express-session`? If so, you'll need to install it:
```
npm install express-session
```

While that installs — let's plan the two files you need to create:

**`src/controllers/auth.js`** — the logic for register and login  
**`src/middleware/auth.js`** — verifies the JWT cookie on protected routes

Here's how JWT auth works in your case:

1. **Register** — hash password with bcrypt, INSERT into users, return 201
2. **Login** — find user, `bcrypt.compare()` password, if ok: `jwt.sign({ userId, username })` → set as `httpOnly` cookie → return user info
3. **Middleware** — read cookie from `req.cookies`, `jwt.verify()` it, attach decoded payload to `req.user`

**Question before you start:** JWT needs a secret key to sign tokens. Where should that secret live? (Hint: think about where `DATABASE_URL` lives)

Good, `jsonwebtoken` is installed. But notice — `cookie-parser` is missing. You need it so Express can read cookies from incoming requests. Install it:

```
npm install cookie-parser
```

While that installs, let me walk you through the plan step by step.

---

### Step 1 — Environment variable for JWT secret

The JWT secret should live in `.env` (same place as `DATABASE_URL`). Add a line like:
```
JWT_SECRET=[REDACTED]
```

**Why?** You never hardcode secrets in source code. The secret is what makes your tokens tamper-proof — if someone knows it, they can forge tokens.

---

### Step 2 — Wire `cookie-parser` into Express

Open `src/app.js` and look at how it's currently set up. You'll need to add `cookie-parser` as middleware with `app.use(cookieParser())` — similar to how you'd add `express.json()`.

**Question:** Do you know where in `app.js` middleware gets registered? Go look at it and tell me what you see.

> AGENT

Good news — `cookie-parser` and `jsonwebtoken` are already installed!

Now, your `dev` script is `node --watch src/server.js` — there's no `--env-file` flag and no `dotenv`. That means `.env` won't be loaded automatically.

Fix that first. Update the `dev` and `start` scripts in `package.json` to:
```
node --env-file=.env --watch src/server.js
node --env-file=.env src/server.js
```

This is Node's built-in `.env` loader (no extra package needed).

---

Then create your `.env` file in the project root with two things:
- Your existing `DATABASE_URL` (check how you connect to postgres — it's whatever you already use)
- A new `JWT_SECRET` — make it a long random string like `mysecretkey123superlong`

---

After that, the build order for Phase 2 is:

1. Add `cookie-parser` to `src/app.js`
2. Create `src/controllers/auth.js` with register + login
3. Add auth routes to `src/router.js`
4. Create `src/middleware/auth.js` to protect routes

Start with `package.json` and `.env` — tell me when done!

> AGENT

You only have the PostgreSQL **client** tools, not the **server**. Install the full server:

```
sudo pacman -S postgresql
```

Then initialize and start it:

```
sudo -u postgres initdb -D /var/lib/postgres/data
sudo systemctl enable --now postgresql
```

Then create your database:

```
sudo -u postgres createdb mal_clone
sudo -u postgres createuser mohmqat --superuser
```

And your `DATABASE_URL` would be:
```
DATABASE_URL=postgres://mohmqat@localhost:5432/mal_clone
```

**Note:** For now, the tests will still pass without a running PostgreSQL — they use PGlite in memory. You only need the real database running for `npm run dev` and `db/build.js`. So we can keep building the auth code in parallel while you sort out the database setup.

Want to continue building `src/app.js` and `src/controllers/auth.js` while PostgreSQL installs?

> AGENT

PostgreSQL still not running — handle that separately.

For `src/app.js`, you need to add two middleware lines before `app.use("/", router)`:

1. `express.json()` — so Express can parse the JSON body from login/register requests (without this, `req.body` is undefined)
2. `cookieParser()` — so Express can read cookies from incoming requests (needed for JWT auth)

Both need to be imported at the top of the file.

**Question:** Do you know the import syntax for `cookie-parser`? Look at how `express` is imported in the same file as a hint — it's the same pattern. The package name is `"cookie-parser"` and you can call the import whatever you like (conventionally `cookieParser`).

Go ahead and update `src/app.js`!

That's correct! Validation is done.

---

**Step 5 — Hash the password and INSERT into the database**

After the validation, you need to:
1. Hash the password with bcrypt
2. Insert the user into the database
3. Return the result

`bcrypt.hash()` returns a Promise. Remember — no async/await, use `.then()`. The chain looks like:

```
bcrypt.hash(password, 10)
  .then((hash) => { ... insert into db ... })
  .then((rows) => { ... send response ... })
  .catch((err) => { ... handle errors ... })
```

For the INSERT, use the same tagged template pattern you used in `db.test.js`:
```
db.sql`INSERT INTO users (username, password_hash) VALUES (${...}, ${...}) RETURNING *`
```

For the response — your test expects 201 with `{ username }` and **no** `password` or `password_hash` in the response. So just send back `{ username: rows[0].username }`.

For the `.catch()` — if the error message contains "unique" or "duplicate", it means the username is taken → return 409. Otherwise return 500.

Write that chain after the validation checks!

> AGENT

Great progress on the structure! The first three lines of the chain are correct. But the `.catch()` has an issue — let me explain:

**Line 19 — the `.catch()` body**

Right now you have:
```js
.catch((err) => {err.message === "unique","duplicate"  })
```

This doesn't actually **do** anything — it just evaluates an expression and throws it away. You need to:
1. **Check** if the error message contains "unique" or "duplicate" (use `.match()` with a regex, or `.includes()`)
2. **If yes** → return 409 with a message like "username already taken"
3. **If no** → return 500 with a generic error

The pattern looks like:
```js
.catch((err) => {
  if (err.message.match(/unique|duplicate/i)) {
    return res.status(409).json({ message: "..." });
  }
  return res.status(500).json({ message: "..." });
})
```

Your test expects the 409 message to match `/taken|already exists/i` — so pick a message that includes one of those words.

Go fix the `.catch()`!

> AGENT

Still one issue — arrow function bodies with statements need curly braces `{ }`. You removed the opening `{` after `err) =>`. Right now you have:

```js
.catch((err) => if (err.message...
```

But it needs to be:

```js
.catch((err) => {
  if (err.message.match(/unique|duplicate/i)) {
    return res.status(409).json({ message: "username already taken" });
  }
  return res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
})
```

Two things to add:
1. `{` right after `(err) =>` 
2. A closing `)` at the very end after the `}` on line 23 (you have `}` but need `})` because `.catch(` opened a paren that needs closing)

Also, the function's closing `}` on line 20 (of your current file) is still there right? Let me re-check — actually I see line 23 ends with `}` but we need `})` to close the `.catch(` call, and then the function's own `}` after.

Fix and recheck!

Good — function closes on line 44.

---

**Step 4 — Compare the password with bcrypt**

If we found a user (`rows.length !== 0`), we need to check if the password matches. `bcrypt.compare(plainPassword, hashFromDB)` returns a Promise that resolves to `true` or `false`.

The tricky part: we want to chain this into the current Promise chain. When you `return` a Promise from inside a `.then()`, the next `.then()` receives the result of that new Promise.

So the pattern is:
```js
.then((rows) => {
  if (rows.length === 0) {
    return res.status(401).json({ message: "invalid credentials" });
  }
  // Store the user for later in the chain
  return bcrypt.compare(password, rows[0].password_hash)
    .then((isMatch) => {
      // Here you have isMatch (true/false) but you also need rows[0] — how?
    });
})
```

Actually, a simpler approach: do the compare inside the existing `.then()` and handle everything in the nested `.then()`:

```js
.then((rows) => {
  if (rows.length === 0) {
    return res.status(401).json({ message: "invalid credentials" });
  }
  const user = rows[0];
  return bcrypt.compare(password, user.password_hash).then((isMatch) => {
    if (!isMatch) {
      return res.status(401).json({ message: "invalid credentials" });
    }
    // If we get here, login is valid — now we sign a JWT
  });
})
```

For now, add the bcrypt compare and the "wrong password" check. We'll add the JWT signing in the next step.

Try it!

9	        return res.status(400).json({ message: "username is empty" });
10	
11	    }
12	    if (password.length < 8) {
13	        return res.status(400).json({ message: "password is too short" });
14	
15	    }
16	    bcrypt.hash(password, 10)
17	        .then((hash) => { return db.sql`INSERT INTO users (username, password_hash) VALUES (${username}, ${hash}) RETURNING *` })
18	        .then((rows) => { res.status(201).json({ username: rows[0].username }) })

20	            if (err.message.match(/unique|duplicate/i)) {

28	export function login(req, res) {
29	    const { username, password } = req.body;
30	    if (!password) {
31	        return res.status(400).json({ message: "password is missing" });
32	
33	    }
34	
35	
36	    db.sql`SELECT * FROM users WHERE username = ${username}`
37	        .then((rows) => {
38	            if (rows.length === 0) {
39	                return res.status(401).json({ message: "invalid credentials" });
40	            }
41	            const user = rows[0];
42	            return bcrypt.compare(password, user.password_hash).then((isMatch) => {
43	                if (!isMatch) {
44	                    return res.status(401).json({ message: "invalid credentials" });
45	                }
46	                const token = jwt.sign({ username: user.username }, process.env.JWT_SECRET);
47	                res.cookie("token", token, { httpOnly: true });
48	                return res.status(200).json({ username: user.username });
49	            });
50	        })
51	        .catch((err) => {
52	            return res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });

> AGENT

Found it! Here's the issue:

In `setup.js`, the test user is seeded with `password_hash = "hashed_password_123"` — but that's **not a real bcrypt hash**, it's just a literal string.

When your login calls `bcrypt.compare("hashed_password_123", "hashed_password_123")`, bcrypt knows the second argument isn't a valid hash, so it returns `false`.

**Fix:** `setup.js` needs to actually bcrypt-hash the password before inserting. You need to:

1. Import `bcrypt` at the top of `setup.js`
2. In the `beforeEach`, hash `"hashed_password_123"` with bcrypt before the INSERT
3. Use that hash in the INSERT

Since setup.js uses `async/await`, it's straightforward:
```js
const hash = await bcrypt.hash("hashed_password_123", 10);
await pglite.query("INSERT INTO users (username, password_hash) VALUES ($1, $2)", [
  "testuser",
  hash,
]);
```

Go make those changes!

> AGENT

Almost, but the changes need to go **inside** `beforeEach`, not outside. Right now:

1. Lines 22-28 are floating at the top level of the module — they'll try to run once when the file loads, but `pglite` doesn't exist yet. Plus line 23-24 has a broken SQL string (newline in the middle).
2. The actual `beforeEach` on lines 33-36 still uses the plaintext password hash.

**Fix:**
1. **Delete lines 22-28** entirely
2. **Replace lines 33-36** (the original INSERT inside `beforeEach`) to hash first, then use the hash:

```js
const hash = await bcrypt.hash("hashed_password_123", 10);
await pglite.query("INSERT INTO users (username, password_hash) VALUES ($1, $2)", [
  "testuser",
  hash,
]);
```

The hashing needs to happen **inside** `beforeEach` because it runs before each test and needs the fresh `pglite` instance.

Line 9 expects `password_hash` to be the literal string `"hashed_password_123"` — but now it's a real bcrypt hash.

**Fix:** Change line 9 to check that `password_hash` starts with `"$2"` (bcrypt hashes all start with `$2a$`, `$2b$`, or `$2y$`):

```js
expect(rows[0].password_hash).toMatch(/^\$2/);
```

Update that line in `db.test.js`. Then all non-favorites tests should pass.

The middleware file is empty. Let's build it.

**What auth middleware does:**
1. Reads the JWT token from the cookie
2. Verifies it with `jwt.verify(token, secret)` using your JWT_SECRET
3. If valid → attaches the user info to `req.user` and calls `next()` (pass control to the next handler)
4. If missing or invalid → returns 401

Express middleware is just a function with this shape:
```js
export function requireAuth(req, res, next) {
  // ...
}
```

The `next()` is how you tell Express "this is fine, move on to the actual route handler."

---

**Step 3 — Verify the JWT**

Now decode the token. `jwt.verify(token, secret)` checks the signature and returns the payload you signed earlier (remember `{ username }`).

But — if the token is tampered/expired/invalid, `jwt.verify` **throws**. So wrap it in a `try/catch`:

```js
try {
  const payload = jwt.verify(token, process.env.JWT_SECRET);
  req.user = payload;
  next();
} catch (err) {
  return res.status(401).json({ message: "unauthorized" });
}
```

Three things happen there:
- `payload` contains `{ username }` you signed
- `req.user = payload` — attaches it to the request so downstream routes can see who's logged in
- `next()` — passes control to the actual route handler

Add that try/catch after the `if (!token)` check. Tell me when done!

- To find the user's favorites, you'd need to first look up their `id` from the `users` table, or subquery it.

`getFavorites` is done.

---

**Now `addFavorite`** — this is the POST handler.

Look at `favorites.test.js`:
```js
.send({ entity_id: 20, entity_type: "anime", title: "Naruto" })
.expect(201)
.expect((res) => {
  expect(res.body).toMatchObject({
    entity_id: 20,
    entity_type: "anime",
    title: "Naruto",
  });
});
```

And the validation:
- `entity_type` must be `"anime"` or `"manga"` → otherwise 400 with message matching `/entity_type/i`
- `entity_id` is required → otherwise 400

**Plan for `addFavorite`:**
1. Destructure `entity_id`, `entity_type`, `title` from `req.body`
2. Validate `entity_type` → 400 if not `anime`/`manga`
3. Validate `entity_id` → 400 if missing
4. Get username from `req.user.username`
5. INSERT with a subquery for `user_id`:
   ```js
   db.sql`INSERT INTO favorites (user_id, entity_id, entity_type, title)
          VALUES ((SELECT id FROM users WHERE username = ${username}), ${entity_id}, ${entity_type}, ${title})
          RETURNING *`

```js
const username = req.user.username;
db.sql`INSERT INTO favorites (user_id, entity_id, entity_type, title)
       VALUES ((SELECT id FROM users WHERE username = ${username}), ${entity_id}, ${entity_type}, ${title})
       RETURNING *`
  .then((rows) => {

  })
  .catch((err) => {
    res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
  });
```

Notice:
- `user_id` uses a subquery to convert username → id
- `RETURNING *` gives us back the newly inserted row
- `rows[0]` because postgres returns an array

Almost — the column order in the INSERT doesn't match the VALUES:

**Line 26** columns: `(user_id, entity_type, entity_id, title)`  
**Line 27** values:  `(subquery, ${entity_id}, ${entity_type}, ${title})`

INSERT INTO favorites (user_id, entity_id, entity_type, title)

46	    if (rows.length === 0) {
47	      return res.status(404).json({ message: "favorite not found" });
48	    }
49	    res.status(200).json({ message: "favorite deleted" });
50	  })
51	  .catch((err) => {
52	    res.status(500).json({ message: "500 Internal Server Error" });
53	  });
54	  }
```

Favorites controller is done!

---

Now **wire everything into `router.js`**:

1. Import the controller functions:
   ```js
   import { getFavorites, addFavorite, deleteFavorite } from "./controllers/favorites.js";
   ```

2. Import the middleware:
   ```js
   import { requireAuth } from "./middleware/auth.js";
   ```

3. Register three routes — the pattern passes middleware **before** the handler:
   ```js
   router.get("/api/favorites", requireAuth, getFavorites);
   router.post("/api/favorites", requireAuth, addFavorite);
   router.delete("/api/favorites/:id", requireAuth, deleteFavorite);
   ```

Put these **before** `router.use(cacheMiddleware)` so they aren't affected by caching.

Tell me when done!

> AGENT

Found the issue! Look at your router:

```js
router.get("/:entityType/:id", ...)          // catches /api/favorites (entityType="api", id="favorites")
router.post("/api/auth/register", register);
router.post("/api/auth/login", login);
router.get("/api/favorites", ...);            // never reached
```

The route `/:entityType/:id` has exactly 2 path segments — same as `/api/favorites`. So Express matches `/api/favorites` against `/:entityType/:id` first (with `entityType="api"`, `id="favorites"`) and serves the HTML file. That's why you get `{}` instead of a JSON array.

**Fix:** Move the `/api/favorites` routes **above** the `/:entityType/:id` line. The API routes should come first, then the catch-all page routes.

The order should be:
1. API routes (`/api/...`)
2. Page routes (`/search/:entityType`, `/:entityType/:id`)

Go reorder them. Tell me when done!

> AGENT

For Yaak, you'll need 5 requests in this order (auth is cookie-based, so Yaak must keep cookies between requests):

**1. Register** — `POST http://localhost:9080/api/auth/register`
Body (JSON): `{"username":"yaakuser","password":"password123"}`

**2. Login** — `POST http://localhost:9080/api/auth/login`
Body: `{"username":"yaakuser","password":"password123"}`
→ Sets `token` httpOnly cookie.

**3. Add favorite** — `POST http://localhost:9080/api/favorites`
Body: `{"entity_id":21,"entity_type":"anime","title":"One Piece"}`

**4. List** — `GET http://localhost:9080/api/favorites`

**5. Delete** — `DELETE http://localhost:9080/api/favorites/1`

Want me to list the error cases to test too (401, 400, 404)?

Now attach an event listener to the form for the `submit` event.

Two issues:

**1. `addEventListener()` is called with no arguments.** It needs two things:
- The event name (a string) — `"submit"`
- A callback function that runs when the event fires — `(event) => { ... }`

**2. Your `console.log("submitted")` is on its own line outside anything.** Right now it runs *once* when the page loads, not when the form is submitted. It needs to go **inside the callback body**, between the `{` and `}`.

Also: the `event.preventDefault()` call I mentioned — don't forget that one. It goes inside the callback too.

Think of the structure like this:
```
form.addEventListener(EVENT_NAME, (event) => {
    // three things happen here when form submits:
    // 1. prevent default
    // 2. log something
});
```

Syntax error on line 2. Look carefully:

```
form.addEventListener("submit" , (event)) => {
```

You closed `addEventListener`'s parentheses too early, **before** the arrow function. The arrow function `(event) => { ... }` is the second argument to `addEventListener`, so it must live **inside** `addEventListener`'s parentheses.

Structure should be:
```
form.addEventListener("submit", CALLBACK);
```

Where `CALLBACK` is the entire arrow function including its body `{ ... }`. The closing `)` of `addEventListener` goes **after** the closing `}` of the arrow function body.

Fix the parenthesis placement, then show me.

Your editor auto-imported a Node.js internal module. This isn't valid in the browser at all. **Delete this line entirely.**

**3. Line 10 — empty headers:**
```

You need an object with one key-value pair. The key is `"Content-Type"`, the value is the MIME type for JSON. What's the MIME type for JSON? (It's `application/json`.)

**4. Line 11 — `body : json.toString`:**
Two issues here:
- `json` is the bogus import from line 1 — won't exist after you delete that line
- You need to **serialize the JS object** `{ username, password }` into a JSON string. The browser provides a global object for this. What's `JSON.stringify(...)`?

2	const form= document.getElementById("register-form")
3	form.addEventListener("submit" , (event) => {

11	} )

> AGENT

Close! Just line 9 is wrong. You wrote:

```
headers :"Content-Type",application/json  ,
```

`headers` must be an **object** (key-value pairs inside `{ }`), not a comma-separated list. The pattern is:

```
headers: { KEY: VALUE }
```

Where:
- `KEY` is `"Content-Type"` (string, in quotes)
- `VALUE` is `"application/json"` (string, in quotes — `application/json` without quotes is a syntax error because `/` gets interpreted as division)

So `headers` should be a single object with that one key-value pair inside curly braces.

Fix line 9 and show me.

> DEVELOPER

guide me

> AGENT

An object literal in JavaScript has this shape:

```
{ key1: value1, key2: value2 }
```

For example:
```
const user = { name: "Alice", age: 30 };
```

Notice: curly braces around the whole thing, each key-value separated by a **colon** (not a comma), and multiple pairs separated by commas.

Your `headers` needs the same shape, but with **one pair**:
- Key: `"Content-Type"` (string with dash)
- Value: `"application/json"` (string with slash)

So structurally, replace line 9 with a line that looks like:

```
    headers: { KEY: VALUE },
```

Four properties: `method`, `headers`, `body`. `headers` is an object containing the content-type. `body` is the stringified user data.

Fetch call is correct. 

## Step 3e: Handle the response

`fetch` returns a Promise. You now need to chain `.then()` calls to handle the response.

**Recall what the server does** (check `src/controllers/auth.js`):
- Success: `201` with JSON `{ username: "..." }`
- Validation error: `400` with `{ message: "..." }`
- Duplicate username: `409` with `{ message: "..." }`

**Plan:**
1. First `.then((response) => ...)` — check `response.ok`. If it's OK, we want to redirect. If not, we want to read the error message.
2. If not OK, you need to read the body with `response.json()` which returns *another* Promise.

**The tricky bit:** since reading the body is async, one common pattern is to always call `.json()` and then handle both cases in a second `.then`:

```
fetch(...)
    .then((response) => {
        return response.json().then((data) => ({ ok: response.ok, data }));
    })
    .then(({ ok, data }) => {
        if (ok) {
            // redirect to /login/
        } else {
            // show data.message in #error-msg
        }
    })
    .catch((err) => {
        // network errors
    });
```

**Your tasks:**
1. Add the chain above to your fetch call
2. For "redirect", use `window.location.href = "/login/"`
3. For "show error", grab `#error-msg` with `getElementById` and set its `.textContent = data.message`

Don't worry about perfection — get something working, then we'll test it.

> AGENT

Structural bug. Look closely: you chained `.then()` off of `JSON.stringify(...)`, which returns a **string**, not a Promise. Strings don't have `.then()` — this will crash at runtime.

The `.then()` chain belongs on the **fetch call itself**, after `fetch(...)` finishes. That means the `.then()` chain goes **outside** the fetch options object, after the closing `})` of the fetch call.

**Current (broken) structure:**
```
fetch("...", {
    method: "POST",
    headers: { ... },
    body: JSON.stringify({...})
      .then(...)       ← WRONG: chained on a string
      .then(...)
      .catch(...)
})
```

**Correct structure:**
```
fetch("...", {
    method: "POST",
    headers: { ... },
    body: JSON.stringify({...})
})
.then(...)             ← chained on the fetch Promise
.then(...)
.catch(...);
```

Move the `.then/.then/.catch` chain out of the options object. Also:
- **Fill in the placeholder comments.** `// redirect to /login/` should become `window.location.href = "/login/"`. `// show data.message in #error-msg` should become `document.getElementById("error-msg").textContent = data.message;`.

Fix the structure and fill in the placeholders, then show me.

Few issues to fix:

**1. Lines 17-18 — stray dots:** you have `"/login/".` and `data.message;.` — those trailing dots are syntax errors (JS thinks you're trying to access a property of a string/semicolon). Delete both trailing dots.

**2. Missing `else` branch:** right now both the redirect AND the error message are inside the `if (ok)` block. That's wrong — you only redirect on success, and only show the error on failure. You need:

```
if (ok) {
    // redirect
} else {
    // show error message
}
```

Move the error message line into an `else` block.

**3. Line 22 — empty catch:** The `// network errors` comment needs a real action. A minimal approach: log the error so you can see it in the console if it happens. Something like `console.error(err)` is fine for now.

2	document.getElementById("search-form").addEventListener("submit", (e) => {
3	  e.preventDefault();
4	  const q = document.getElementById("text").value;
5	  const type = document.getElementById("type").value;
6	  if (q.trim()) {
7	    window.location.href = `/search/${type}?q=${encodeURIComponent(q)}`;
8	  }
9	});
10	
11	const detailsContainer = document.getElementById("anime-details-container");
12	const id = window.location.pathname.split("/")[2];
13	const entityType = window.location.pathname.split("/")[1];
14	
15	// Set the type selector to match current entity
16	document.getElementById("type").value = entityType;
17	
18	function fetchAnime() {
19	  return fetch(`/api/${entityType}/${encodeURIComponent(id)}`).then((response) => response.json());
20	}
21	
22	function renderAnime(data) {
23	  const d = data.data;
24	  detailsContainer.innerHTML = "";
25	
26	  const card = document.createElement("div");
27	  card.className = "detail-card";
28	
29	  // Cover image (right side)
30	  const cover = document.createElement("div");
31	  cover.className = "detail-cover";
32	  const img = document.createElement("img");
33	  img.src = d.images.jpg.large_image_url || d.images.jpg.image_url;
34	  img.alt = d.title;
35	  cover.appendChild(img);
36	
37	  // Info (left side)
38	  const info = document.createElement("div");
39	  info.className = "detail-info";
40	
41	  const title = document.createElement("h1");
42	  title.className = "detail-title";
43	  title.textContent = d.title;
44	
45	  // Meta tags
46	  const metaGrid = document.createElement("div");
47	  metaGrid.className = "detail-meta-grid";
48	
49	  const tags = [
50	    { label: "Score", value: d.score ?? "N/A" },
51	    { label: "Type", value: d.type || "Unknown" },
52	    { label: "Status", value: d.status || "Unknown" },
53	  ];
54	
55	  if (entityType === "anime") {
56	    tags.push({ label: "Episodes", value: d.episodes ?? "?" });
57	  } else {
58	    tags.push({ label: "Chapters", value: d.chapters ?? "?" });
59	    tags.push({ label: "Volumes", value: d.volumes ?? "?" });
60	  }
61	
62	  if (d.year) tags.push({ label: "Year", value: d.year });
63	  if (d.rating) tags.push({ label: "Rating", value: d.rating });
64	
65	  tags.forEach((t) => {
66	    const tag = document.createElement("span");
67	    tag.className = "detail-tag";
68	    tag.innerHTML = `<strong>${t.label}:</strong> ${t.value}`;
69	    metaGrid.appendChild(tag);
70	  });
71	
72	  // Genres
73	  const genres = document.createElement("div");
74	  genres.className = "detail-genres";
75	  if (d.genres) {
76	    d.genres.forEach((g) => {
77	      const badge = document.createElement("span");
78	      badge.className = "genre-badge";
79	      badge.textContent = g.name;
80	      genres.appendChild(badge);
81	    });
82	  }
83	
84	  // Synopsis
85	  const synopsisLabel = document.createElement("div");
86	  synopsisLabel.className = "detail-synopsis-label";
87	  synopsisLabel.textContent = "Synopsis";
88	
89	  const synopsis = document.createElement("div");
90	  synopsis.className = "detail-synopsis";
91	  synopsis.textContent = d.synopsis || "No synopsis available.";
92	
93	  info.appendChild(title);
94	  info.appendChild(metaGrid);
95	  info.appendChild(genres);
96	  info.appendChild(synopsisLabel);
97	  info.appendChild(synopsis);
98	
99	  card.appendChild(info);
100	  card.appendChild(cover);
101	
102	  detailsContainer.appendChild(card);
103	}
104	
105	fetchAnime().then(renderAnime);
106	
```

> AGENT

Good start — you've got both elements. Now add the fetch call.

## Step 3: Fetch the favorites

Right after those two lines, call `fetch("/api/favorites")`. Since this is a GET request, you **don't need** a second argument (no method, no headers, no body — the browser defaults to GET).

Chain a `.then()` on it. Inside that `.then()`, you receive `response`. You need to handle two cases:

**If the user is NOT logged in**, the server returns 401. How do you check that? `response.ok` is `false` when the status is 4xx. So:
- If `!response.ok` → set `errMsg.textContent` to something like `"Please log in to see favorites"` and **return** (stop the chain)
- If `response.ok` → return `response.json()` to parse the body (this gives the next `.then()` the data array)

Then add a second `.then((data) => { ... })` where you just `console.log(data)` for now.

And a `.catch()` at the end for network errors.

**The structure looks like:**

```
fetch(URL)
  .then((response) => {
      if (!response.ok) {
          // show error, return
      }
      return response.json()
  })
  .then((data) => {
      // log data for now
  })
  .catch((err) => {
      // handle error
  })
```

Write it and show me.

> AGENT

Three issues to fix:

**1. Line 1** — `const { response } = require("express")`. Your editor auto-imported again. This is a browser file, not Node.js. `require` doesn't exist in the browser. **Delete this line entirely.**

**2. Line 9** — `errMsg.textContent("please log in...")`. `textContent` is a **property**, not a function. You don't call it with parentheses — you assign to it with `=`. Also, you need to `return` after this line so the chain stops and the second `.then()` doesn't run.

**3. Line 12** — `return response.json`. You're missing the parentheses — `json` is a **method** that needs to be called: `response.json()`.

**4. Lines 18-19** — `response.status(500).json(...)`. That's server-side Express code — this is a frontend file. In the `.catch()`, just do `console.error(err)` like you did on the other pages.

Fix all four and show me.

> AGENT

Good progress — the guards are in place. Two things to fix/add:

**1. Line 15** — `return(errMsg)` just returns the HTML element, which does nothing useful. You need to **set the text** on it, like you did on line 7. Set `errMsg.textContent = "no favorites yet"` and then `return`.

**2. The forEach loop is missing.** After the empty check, you need to loop through `data` and render each favorite. Here's the approach, step by step:

**Step A:** Start the loop:
```
data.forEach((fav) => {
    
})
```

**Step B:** Inside the loop, create a `<div>` element:
- `document.createElement("div")` — store it in a variable like `card`

**Step C:** Put the favorite's title inside the div:
- `card.textContent = fav.title` — each item in `data` has a `.title` property

**Step D:** Append the card to the container:
- `favourite.appendChild(card)` — adds it to the page

That's it for now — just title text in a div. We'll add the delete button and links after this works.

Fix line 15 and add the forEach loop, then show me.

1	const favourite = document.getElementById("favorites-container");
2	const errMsg = document.getElementById("error-msg");
3	fetch("/api/favorites")
4	  .then((response) => {
5	    if (!response.ok) {
6	      errMsg.textContent = "please log in to see favorites ";
7	      return;
8	    }
9	    return response.json();
10	  })
11	  .then((data) => {
12	    console.log(data);
13	    if (!data) return;
14	    if (data.length === 0) {
15	      errMsg.textContent = "no favorites yet";
16	      return;
17	    }
18	    data.forEach((fav) => {
19	      const card=document.createElement("div")
20	      card.textContent=fav.title
21	      favourite.appendChild(card)
22	    });
23	  })
24	  .catch((err) => {
25	    console.log(err);
26	  });
27

> AGENT

Clean. The basic favorites page is working — fetch, guard checks, empty state, and rendering titles.

Now let's make each card more useful. Two additions:

**1. A link to the detail page** — so clicking a favorite takes you to its detail page. Each favorite has `entity_type` (anime/manga) and `entity_id`. Your detail page URL pattern is `/:entityType/:id`.

Instead of setting `card.textContent`, create an `<a>` element inside the card. What `href` should it have? Think about what URL takes you to the detail page for a specific anime/manga. (Hint: use `fav.entity_type` and `fav.entity_id`.)

**2. A delete button** — so the user can remove a favorite. Create a `<button>` element with text "Delete", and append it to the card.

Don't wire the delete button to fetch yet — just make it appear. We'll add the click handler next.

Write both additions inside the `.forEach()` loop and show me.

guide me

> AGENT

Inside your `.forEach((fav) => { ... })` loop, you currently have 3 lines. Keep the `createElement("div")` and `appendChild(card)` but replace `card.textContent = fav.title` with two new elements inside the card:

## Element 1: The link

**Step A:** Create an `<a>` element:
```
document.createElement("a")
```
Store it in a variable like `link`.

**Step B:** Set its text to the favorite's title:
- `link.textContent = fav.title`

**Step C:** Set its `href` — where should it go? Your detail page lives at `/:entityType/:id`. Each favorite has `fav.entity_type` and `fav.entity_id`. So the href should be a string that combines those two into a URL path like `/anime/21` or `/manga/13`.

**Step D:** Append the link to the card:
- `card.appendChild(link)`

## Element 2: The delete button

**Step E:** Create a `<button>` element. Store it in a variable like `btn`.

**Step F:** Set its text: `btn.textContent = "Delete"`

**Step G:** Append the button to the card:
- `card.appendChild(btn)`

So your loop should have: create div, create link, create button, append link to div, append button to div, append div to container.

Write it and show me.

> AGENT

Four issues:

**1. Line 1** — Auto-import strikes again. `require("express")` doesn't work in the browser. **Delete line 1.**

**2. Line 29** — Two problems with the fetch call:
- You used regular quotes `"..."` but you need **backticks** `` `...` `` for template literals. With quotes, `${fav.id}` is literal text, not a variable.
- The second argument to `fetch` should be an **options object** with a `method` property, not just the string `"Delete"`. It should look like: `{ method: "DELETE" }`

So the fetch call needs to be:
```
fetch(`/api/favorites/${fav.id}`, { method: "DELETE" })
```

**3. Lines 34-36** — `.catch()` is **inside** the `.then()` callback. It needs to be **chained after** it. The `.then()` closing `})` must come before `.catch()`.

Current (broken):
```
.then((response) => {
    if (response.ok) {
        card.remove()
    }
    .catch(...)    ← inside .then()
})
```

Correct:
```
.then((response) => {
    if (response.ok) {
        card.remove()
    }
})
.catch(...)        ← chained after .then()
```

Fix all four and show me.

The `.catch` chaining is fixed. But **line 28 still has the same two problems** from before:

```
fetch("/api/favorites/${fav.id","Delete")
```

**Problem 1:** You're using regular quotes `"..."`. With regular quotes, `${fav.id}` is treated as literal text, not a variable. Switch to **backticks**: `` ` ` ``

**Problem 2:** `"Delete"` is a plain string. `fetch` expects an **object** as the second argument. Wrap it: `{ method: "DELETE" }`

Line 28 should look like:

```
fetch(`/api/favorites/${fav.id}`, { method: "DELETE" })
```

Notice: backticks around the URL, and `{ method: "DELETE" }` as the options object.

 **Proxy pattern**: frontend never calls Jikan directly. All API calls go through the Express server.
 
-| Express route            | Jikan endpoint    |
-| ------------------------ | ----------------- |
-| `/api/top-anime`         | `GET /v4/top/anime` |
+| Express route            | Jikan endpoint            |
+| ------------------------ | ------------------------- |
+| `/api/top-anime`         | `GET /v4/top/anime`       |
 | `/api/search?q=...`      | `GET /v4/anime?q={query}` |
-| `/api/anime-details/:id` | `GET /v4/anime/{id}` |
+| `/api/anime-details/:id` | `GET /v4/anime/{id}`      |
 
 - Routes use kebab-case
 - Detail page URL: `/anime/:id` — Express serves `public/detail/index.html` via manual route in `router.js`

+

 - Anime array at `response.data`
 - Each anime: `title`, `score`, `mal_id`, `images.jpg.image_url`

 # Week 6+7 TODO — PostgreSQL, Auth, Favorites, Testing
 
 ## Phase 1 — Database Foundation (Week 6)
+
 - [x] 1. Set up PostgreSQL locally and create the project database
 - [x] 2. Design schema: `users` table and `favorites` table (with `entity_type` column to distinguish anime vs manga) with foreign key relationship
 - [x] 3. Write `db/build.sql` with CREATE TABLE statements
@@ -9,6 +10,7 @@
 - [x] 6. Use parameterized queries (`$1, $2`) in all database queries
 
 ## Phase 2 — Authentication (Week 7)
+
 - [ ] 7. Create `POST /api/register` — validate inputs, hash password with bcrypt, INSERT into users
 - [ ] 8. Create `POST /api/login` — bcrypt compare, set cookie or JWT on success
 - [ ] 9. Add server-side validation (username not empty, password min length, duplicate username check)
@@ -16,6 +18,7 @@
 - [ ] 11. Build auth middleware that checks cookie/JWT on protected routes
 
 ## Phase 3 — Favorites Feature (Week 6 + 7 combined)
+
 - [ ] 12. Create `POST /api/favorites` (protected) — save an anime/manga to favorites
 - [ ] 13. Create `GET /api/favorites` (protected) — fetch user's saved favorites
 - [ ] 14. Create `DELETE /api/favorites/:id` (protected) — remove a favorite
@@ -23,6 +26,7 @@
 - [ ] 16. Build a "My Favorites" page to display saved anime/manga
 
 ## Phase 4 — Testing (Week 6 + 7)
+
 - [x] 17. Set up a separate test database
 - [x] 18. Test database queries (INSERT, SELECT, DELETE for users and favorites)
 - [x] 19. Test auth routes (register, login, invalid credentials)

## Step 1: Create the button

Inside `renderAnime`, after line 97 (`info.appendChild(synopsis)`) and before line 99 (`card.appendChild(info)`), you need to:

1. Create a `<button>` element
2. Set its text to something like `"Save to Favorites"`
3. Append it to `info` (so it appears below the synopsis)

**Question before you write:** Look at the `renderAnime` function. You already have access to three pieces of data you'll need for the `POST /api/favorites` request:
- `entityType` (line 13) — "anime" or "manga"
- `id` (line 12) — the entity ID from the URL
- `d.title` (line 43) — the anime/manga title

> AGENT

**Line 1** — auto-import again. `require("express")` in a browser file. Delete it.

**Lines 104-119** — the structure is broken. Let me explain what went wrong:

The fetch options object (starting line 104) was never closed before `.then()`. Also, `.then((response) => { ... })` has literal `{ ... }` instead of real code, and the if/else is floating outside the `.then()` callback.

Here's the structure you need. Look at how things close:

```
fetch("/api/favorites", {           ← opens fetch options
    method: "POST",
    headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
    body: JSON.stringify({          ← opens stringify
        entity_id: id,
        entity_type: entityType,
        title: d.title,
    })                              ← closes stringify
})                                  ← closes fetch options
.then((response) => {               ← opens .then callback
    if (response.ok) {
        btn.textContent = "Saved!"
    } else {
        btn.textContent = "Failed"
    }
})                                  ← closes .then callback
.catch((err) => {
    console.error(err)
})
```

Key points:
- `})` closes the fetch options **before** `.then()` starts
- The if/else goes **inside** the `.then()` callback, not outside it
- No `{ ... }` placeholder — write the actual code

Fix lines 1 and 104-119, then show me.

Only client-side validation remains. Want to continue or stop here?

> DEVELOPER

What validate it is ?

> AGENT

Client-side validation means checking the user's input **in the browser before sending it to the server**. Right now your register/login forms send the data no matter what — even if fields are empty.

Your server already validates (in `src/controllers/auth.js`):
- Empty username → 400
- Password < 8 chars → 400

Client-side validation would do the **same checks in `app.js`** before calling `fetch`, so the user gets instant feedback without waiting for a server round-trip.

For example, in `public/register/app.js`, right after `preventDefault()` and reading the input values, you could check:
- Is username empty? → show error in `#error-msg`, don't fetch
- Is password shorter than 8 characters? → show error, don't fetch

It's a nice UX improvement but **not required** — the server catches these errors already. Want to add it or skip it?

> DEVELOPER

Mentor here, think whether zod validation should extend to do async validation of added parameters (anime id existing before passing into controller?)

> AGENT

Good question. Yes, Zod validation could extend to async validation — checking whether an `entity_id` actually exists in Jikan before saving it to favorites. This would prevent users from saving garbage IDs.

**However, there are trade-offs to consider:**

**For async validation (check Jikan before saving):**
- Prevents invalid anime/manga IDs in the favorites table
- Data integrity — every favorite links to a real entity
- Catches typos or malicious input early

**Against async validation:**
- Adds latency to every POST /api/favorites (extra HTTP call to Jikan)
- Jikan has rate limits — validating on every save could trigger 429s
- Jikan could be down — should saving a favorite fail because an external API is unreachable?
- The detail page already fetched the anime data to render it, so the ID is almost certainly valid by the time the user clicks "Save"

**My recommendation:** Keep it as a **synchronous Zod schema** for now (validate shape: `entity_id` is a positive integer, `entity_type` is "anime" or "manga", `title` is a non-empty string). The async Jikan check adds complexity and external dependency to a write path that's already gated behind authentication. If data integrity becomes a real concern later, it could be added as a separate middleware.

Does the mentor want the student to implement the async validation as a learning exercise, or keep it to synchronous Zod schemas?

> DEVELOPER

synchronus zod validation, but add checks inside the controller for the validity of the inputs (you should already do that to fetch the title, not trust user input)

> AGENT

Got it. So the approach is:

1. **Zod** validates the shape — `entity_id` is a positive integer, `entity_type` is `"anime"` or `"manga"`
2. **Controller** fetches Jikan with the validated `entity_id` + `entity_type` to verify it exists **and** gets the real title from Jikan — never trust the user-submitted `title`

This means `title` gets removed from the request body entirely — the server fetches it itself. Good security pattern.

Let me update the TODO to reflect this.
